National League clubs interested as 31-year-old leaves Millwall by mutual consent

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Millwall have had experienced goalkeeper Joel Coleman on the books as cover since the summer of 2025. He is yet to make an appearance for the Lions and was left out of their 25-man EFL squad for the ongoing campaign.

Now, writing on X, reporter Pete O’Rourke states that Coleman and the club have come to an agreement over the mutual termination of his contract. It frees the 31-year-old to depart and find a new team outside of the transfer window, and there are National League sides keen on the shot-stopper.

Coleman has played the majority of football across his career in League One, but he has appeared in League Two and the Championship too. He was in three matchday squads for Alex Neil‘s side this season prior to his omission.

Including Coleman, Millwall have had five goalkeepers on their books this season. Even with Lukas Jensen and Filip Marschall cast to the sidelines through injury, the former Bolton Wanderers and Huddersfield Town man has been third choice behind Max Crocombe and George Evans for the most part.

Injuries can bring opportunities for the backup ‘keepers, but the Lions’ depth in this position means it would take a serious crisis, dismal form from several players or both for Coleman to come into contention for minutes in Neil’s side.

It means the Championship club can afford to let him go mid-season without signing a replacement. Coleman joined on a free transfer as well, so they’re not taking a big loss in terminating his contract either.

It remains to be seen just where he winds up after his contract end, but this move should benefit Coleman.

Coleman has spent much of his career as a backup goalkeeper, but his minutes have dried up in recent times especially. He never played for Millwall and he didn’t appear in his final season at Bolton Wanderers in 2024/25 either.

Having kept training, the Bolton-born shot-stopper will hope he can move to a National League club and quickly become a hit. He’ll have plenty of experience and pedigree to offer so could become an influential asset on and off the pitch.

Coleman’s best season came as he broke through at Oldham Athletic, keeping 12 clean sheets and conceding 35 goals in 32 League One appearances across the 2015/16 season. He will hope to tap back into that form in the bid to become a starting shot-stopper again.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Cheated On His Marriage With An Alleged Spy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Happy Monday! It's November 9, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair, and rough times over at Tesla and SpaceX. We'll also look at dealerships sharing your most sensitive conversations with AI, and Toyota trying to get a break on tariffs.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Jensen Huang, Nvidia CEO and leather jacket enthusiast, cheated on his wife. This is a simple fact, admitted by Huang. The part of the situation that's still up for debate is whether the woman Huang cheated with is a Chinese spy sent to gather intel on Nvidia's precious advanced micro devices. If so, it could spell trouble — not just for Nvidia, not just for autonomous vehicles, but for everything wrapped up in the AI bubble. From Bloomberg:

Nvidia Corp.'s investigation into Chief Executive Officer Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy raises a series of once-unthinkable questions, including whether the man at the center of the AI boom is at risk of losing his job.

Nvidia shares slid about 5% in premarket trading after Huang disclosed the romantic relationship in a memo to employees, a decline that would mean a roughly $225 billion loss in market capitalization. The company's board is assembling a committee of independent directors who will work with outside counsel to investigate the matter.

With the stunning revelation, investors need to consider a range of possibilities that could affect not just Huang and Nvidia, but the entire artificial intelligence industry. Nvidia, the leading maker of AI chips, has used its financial strength to back dozens of major players, from frontier labs like OpenAI to cloud computing players like CoreWeave Inc. Huang has also been among the most forceful advocates for the benefits of AI, from Washington to Wall Street.

If there's one thing that rich guys love more than acting incredibly divorced, it's cheating on their spouses in order to get divorced in the first place. There's a possible trajectory here where Jensen Huang's inability to keep it in his pants crashes the global economy — the AI bubble, its circular financing, and everything invested in it all comes tumbling down.

Tesla and SpaceX have been teasing a merger for a while now, bringing CEO Elon Musk's biggest companies — Tesla, SpaceX, Twitter, xAI — under one cult of personality stock ticker. Shares have been climbing in anticipation of such a merger, but the midterm blue wave likely took that off the table. Now, investors are balking. From Reuters:

Nov 5 (Reuters) – Shares of Elon Musk's companies ‌SpaceX and Tesla slumped on Thursday, extending losses from the previous session, as investors worried that Democratic wins in the US midterm elections could slow data center build-outs.

SpaceX, which has leaned into the ​AI data center boom, is headquartered in Texas alongside Tesla, where Democrats won ​the races for Senate, governor and attorney general on Tuesday. Musk ⁠had spent heavily to back Republicans in the midterms.

The selloff also reflects worries that ​Democratic gains could bring tougher scrutiny of Musk's businesses.

Tesla has recovered some since this Reuters piece — it's up a bit over 5% today as of this writing — but it's still down from pre-midterm highs. SpaceX is on a near-identical trajectory, but slightly higher. Don't worry, Elon still has money.

Are you concerned that AI might not be invading enough of your private life? Well, now you can rest easy with the knowledge that dealerships are bringing it in — not just on the sales floor, but in the F&I box. Some dealers are recording those conversations, and running them through AI in order to upsell you more effectively. From Automotive News:

Until this year, Len Stoler Automotive Group's finance offices generally sold one product per vehicle.

Now, that number is closer to two. The group says employees — coached in part by artificial intelligence — deserve some of the credit.

Len Stoler Automotive uses AI-powered software to record sales interactions with customers. Part training tool and part compliance coach, the technology provides feedback to the group's sales teams to help them highlight the value of a product, rather than push a customer to buy more.

Auto News frames this as a positive, a way for dealers to make more money, while to me it's horrifying. Yeah, why not share all my most private financial information with an AI in real time — an AI with the explicit intent of upselling me on stuff I don't need? Even if it doesn't work, which is likely, that AI now has my financial data to lose in some data breach down the line. Cut it out, dealers.

When Audi promised to build a factory in the U.S., it got a tariff discount from the Trump administration in exchange. Now Toyota, like a middle child feeling left out, is asking if that means it can have extra dessert too. From Automotive News:

When the Trump administration reportedly floated some level of tariff relief to Audi before the election in exchange for a commitment to build an assembly plant in Georgia, no one was more interested than Toyota Motor Corp.

The Japanese automaker paid more than $8 billion in tariffs during its last fiscal year, which ended March 31, dwarfing the bill from every other automaker struggling with the duties President Donald Trump imposed in early 2025.

Now, as Toyota works feverishly to lower the tariff bill, it is asking the Commerce Department whether the preelection offer floated to Audi for its $4 billion plant in Georgia also applies to Toyota because of the $3.6 billion factory it said in July it would build in Texas.

Toyota doesn't have the best bargaining position here — it already agreed to build a Texas factory, without a tariff deal in exchange — but it can't hurt the company to try. The Trump administration is on the back foot now, and it might be more willing to make deals in exchange for good press.

We're nearly back below $4 per gallon, which seemed impossible just months ago. Is this the power of Woke?

Daniel Craig's Bond movies ended with No Time to Die, opening the door for a new actor to take on the mantle in the developing 26th movie by Denis Villeneuve. Fans have spent years taking bets on the next Bond, and the Oscar-nominated filmmaker recently trolled us at a recent event.

No Time to Die's ending shockingly killed off 007, a first for the long-running franchise (which is streaming with an Amazon Prime subscription). Those involved with the next movie have been keeping tight-lipped about casting, but Villeneuve spoke at the LACMA Art + Film Gala, where he spoke to the crowd and said:

With the blessing of my new friends at Amazon, I will reveal to you tonight who will be the next James Bond. The actor who will play the next James Bond is… And I will stop right now because Amy Pascal is about to have a heart attack.

What a tease. Clearly the acclaimed director knows that folks are dying to know who will take on the role of 007 in his James Bond movie. And what's a little light trolling among friends? Hopefully we'll get more answers about his developing blockbuster once Dune: Part Three hits theaters this December as half of Dunesday.

Denis Villeneuve's involvement in Bond 26 helping to buoy excitement for the mysterious project... even if we still don't know who will be playing the lead role. In a video from Variety's Instagram, he got honest about why he can't say much right now, offering:

I should not comment right now because every word I’m going to say is going to be scrutinized. I would just say that I’m having a great time.

The Arrival director was being cheeky at the event, but these comments show a much more sincere side of him. He's clearly thrilled to be behind the camera for the next James Bond movie, even if fans are desperate for any information regarding casting. You can see a video of him giving the above quote below:

It's been years since No Time to Die hit theaters back in 2021, fans have been curious about which actor will be passed the baton from Daniel Craig. Unfortunately we're still waiting on casting news, with fans tossing around a number of familiar names online. There are tons of rumors about who might be playing the martini-sipping agent, as well as chatter that the studio might go with an unknown rather than an established actor. Hopefully we get some answers soon.

All of the Bond movies are streaming on Amazon, and Denis Villeneuve's Dune: Part Three hits theaters December 18th as part of the 2026 movie release list. Fingers crossed that more Bond 26 info comes after that title gets its wide theatrical release.

Tottenham seek ban on journalist over anti-Spurs chants with Arsenal fans

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Tottenham, featuring team news and expert analysis from Sam Tabuteau

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Tottenham have lodged a formal complaint to the Premier League about the actions of a reporter working for ESPN Brasil.

Joao Castelo-Branco, who works as an English football correspondent for ESPN, was seen in video footage shared on his personal social media taking part in anti-Tottenham chanting with Arsenal fans in Sao Paulo.

The video was shared on Castelo-Branco’s personal Instagram page as well as an account belonging to an ESPN podcast and has now led to Spurs filing an official complaint against the journalist.

As part of their complaint, Tottenham have asked the Premier League to prevent Castelo-Branco from covering the club in future. That includes attending matches, press conferences and potential interviews.

The exclusions would not affect any other reporter or the broadcaster themselves, with ESPN a Premier League rights holder in the Caribbean and South America.

Castelo-Branco apologised for his actions in an interview with The Times and said the videos had since been removed from both social media accounts.

“I wanted to apologise to the club for the video I recorded with Arsenal fans in Brazil singing a derogatory chant about Tottenham Hotspur,” Castelo-Branco said. “Given my role as a correspondent in the UK, I feel I made a mistake.

“The video was posted on the podcast Instagram page and reproduced on my personal page. I understand it can be offensive to Spurs and Spurs fans and I should have thought more about singing that and the reach it could have.

“ESPN was notified by the Premier League about Tottenham seeing and disliking the post, and we agreed to delete it. Hopefully we can move on, and I will rethink and be more careful with the boundaries of having that persona on the podcast.”

Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Lawsuit is filed over shirtless Bill Belichick video

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On Saturday, former Patriots coach Bill Belichick returned to New England, for a game between North Carolina and Connecticut. One day earlier, a lawsuit over a leaked video of a shirtless Belichick was filed in Massachusetts.

Via Edward Lewis of the California Post, Jordon Hudson (who either is dating or was dating Belichick) sued the owners of an Airbnb rental for disclosing the Ring camera video generated three years ago, when Hudson and Belichick were using the property.

The legal argument seems to be that the owners, by disclosing the video of Belichick sans shirt, triggered a storm of unflattering scrutiny of Hudson.

From the lawsuit: “As a result of this leak, numerous false tabloid-like and click-bait stories have, and continue to, be published about her, which have held her up to intense ridicule and derision and caused immense damage.”

The litigation seeks, among other things, compensation for “invasion of privacy and breach of contract” and an order prohibiting the property owners “from disclosing or disseminating any personal and/or private information related to Ms. Hudson to any third-party.”

The entire premise of the lawsuit seems to be that the leak “outed [Hudson’s] romantic relationship with Belichick, something she had worked hard to keep private.” One of the basic factual questions will be whether she behaved in a manner consistent with keeping her identity as Belichick’s secret girlfriend private.

At some point, she wasn’t keeping it private. She was flaunting it.

By filing the lawsuit, Hudson has pulled the pin on a grenade that may eventually throw shrapnel at Belichick. If, as it appears, she believes that she never would have gotten her 15 minutes (and then some) of infamy without the video of shirtless Bill being leaked, it will be fair for the defendants’ lawyers to aggressively explore all of the ways in which she seemingly sought out attention and/or notoriety.

The lawsuit ultimately sets the stage for a potentially aggressive discovery process that will most likely include lengthy depositions of Hudson and Belichick.

And, during the lawyer-controlled question-and-answer sessions, Hudson will not be able to blurt out, “We’re not answering that.”

As it relates to Belichick, the lawsuit creates potential hassle, headache, and distraction. He should have tried to talk her out of doing it.

Maybe he did. If he did, he failed miserably.

Give Onion Rings A Protein Boost With This Breakfast Side

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Pairing your onion rings with a meaty ingredient doesn't just infuse them with umami, but can also make them a meal in their own right. Bacon-wrapped onion rings, in particular, are relatively straightforward to put together and have roughly 3 to 4 grams of protein per piece (depending on how much bacon each ring uses).

Onions already have a dose of fiber, potassium, and vitamins C and B6, so adding some extra protein can give you something with a more comprehensive nutritional profile. While exact quantities vary based on the type, cut, and marbling of the bacon you use, meat is a "complete protein," meaning it contains all nine essential amino acids necessary for good health. Since this recipe doesn't call for deep-frying, it's not only easier, but also a good alternative for those attempting to reduce their consumption of vegetable-based oils. You won't even have to make sure any breading sticks, so it's a protein-laden option for those who want a bit of indulgence but steer clear of starches and grains.

The recommended minimum dietary allowance for protein is 0.36 grams per pound of body weight, so getting your necessary amount every day can be a challenge. Finding little ways to introduce more into your diet, especially complete proteins, is a solid way to achieve this. Since leftover bacon reheats quite well, it's easy to make a batch of these onion rings ahead of time and have little protein snacks available throughout the week!

Not all bacon is made equally, but you don't necessarily have to use the high-end slabs you can only find at a butcher shop. Just about anything will work, but some types are better for this recipe than others, and finding ways to use what you have is essential to tweaking it to your palate.

When it comes to center versus thick-cut bacon, you may be better off using something on the thinner side. Even turkey bacon is a solid choice, with similar protein and less fat, but only if it's easy to work with. Cooking times vary quite a bit depending on your method — this could take 10 to 15 minutes in the air fryer, or up to an hour and a half on the grill if you're cooking over low heat — but you only have two real goals for this recipe. The first is giving the bacon a nice, crispy texture. The second is bringing the meat to a safe level of doneness, following the package instructions for pork or turkey bacon.

If you're grilling, a toothpick or skewer is the best way to ensure your floppy bacon sticks to the onion ring, but your food can still be a bit delicate to move once it comes to room temperature. A quick trip to the fridge can help the meat stiffen enough to move onto the grill, where it should crisp and harden enough that it won't fall off quite so easily. This is also a good opportunity to let any additional seasoning soak into the meat, better infusing it with flavor.

NCIS Series Confirms The Early End Of Its 2026 Run

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ana Dumaraog is a Writer and Editor at Screen Rant, where she expertly covers Network TV offerings, among other things She has spearheaded the effort to make the site an authority on broadcast shows, initially starting with The Big Bang Theory franchise, NCIS, and One Chicago before taking on the specialization in general. Ana has also covered a wide-spread of topics in the past — from franchises like, the MCU and Star Wars, to classic TV favorites, such as Frasier and MASH.

Ana brings clarity and enthusiasm to every story she tackles—whether reporting on new releases or offering deep dives into fan-favorite franchises. Her coverage translates complex entertainment developments into engaging, audience-focused content.

One NCIS series is moving forward with its early 2026 ending.

CBS's revamped Super Tuesday has become an important part of this network TV's cycle. Each show in the three-hour block has given the massive fandom something to look forward to — whether that's the beginning of a new era for NCIS in New York or the revelation about Lala's present-day fate. Each team has been dealing with their own weekly cases, but there's increased focus on its serialized stories, as they have a bigger impact on the franchise's overall lore.

That's exactly the case for NCIS: Origins season 3, which has seen the full-time return of Mark Harmon as Leroy Jethro Gibbs. While the actor has already been involved in the project, providing the narration for every episode on top of producing it, this marks the first time that he is appearing throughout the cycle as Old Gibbs. That comes with a contemporary story that has him reuniting with his former agents: Tony Dinozzo Jr. and Ellie Bishop. Meanwhile, NCIS: Origins' period narrative plays parallel it.

With just four episodes out, Lala actor, Mariel Molino, has confirmed that NCIS: Origins season 3 production is down to filming their last episode. On her Instagram, which has been shared by the official NCIS account, she shares that they have only three more days left of shooting via a behind-the-scenes image of her and Caleb Foote getting ready as their respective characters. Check out the image below:

For context, CBS previously confirmed that NCIS: Origins season 3 would have a shortened run of just 10 episodes. This is what allowed the show to tackle more serialized stories, with most of its weekly cases tying into a bigger narrative decades into the future. That said, there has been no additional specific timeline information offered until Molino's post, which confirms that they are near the end of production for the season. Beyond that, the fate of NCIS: Origins is still in limbo, as CBS has yet to renew it for season 4.

Whether showrunner David J. North is preparing for the possibility that this will be the final run for the prequel is curious. Granted that the focus of its current year has been answering major questions about this era in Gibbs' life, NCIS: Origins still has so much story left to tell outside of that. Throughout its run, it has done a great job endearing other supporting cast members to the public, and it would be a shame not to learn more about them, especially those who haven't had any presence in the flagship. It would also be great to see Gibbs' undercover era — something that NCIS established during Jenny Shepard's time as the agency's director.

Six years after their final appearance, the original young Gibbs is officially recast in the NCIS franchise playing a more interesting character.

No specific date has been confirmed with regard to when NCIS: Origins season 3 will wrap up its run as part of CBS's fall 2026 schedule. That said, it's possible that all 10 episodes will already be out before the network enters its mandatory holiday hiatus for 2026. After that, the long wait for its future verdict will officially begin. For now, the public still has six episodes left to look forward to.

F1 in Brazil: You’ve got to keep your tires alive

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

A topsy-turvy grid and intermittent rain resulted in a very entertaining race.

In sports, a “dead rubber” is a match that takes place after the outcome of the series or championship has already been decided. The people in charge hate this*—if you wrap up a title two-thirds of the way through a season, the viewers evaporate, as there’s nothing left at stake. But anyone who decided to skip this year’s Brazilian Grand Prix this past weekend missed one of the year’s most entertaining races, as a combination of a mixed-up starting order, some very fast drivers at the back, and a wet track that dried and then saw rain again resulted in action aplenty.

A peculiarity of F1 is that even if you manage to win a championship, there’s no guarantee you’ll be in a position to defend it. Take McLaren’s Lando Norris, who took the crown in 2025 after a nail-biting fight with his teammate Oscar Piastri and Red Bull’s Max Verstappen. Sadly for Lando, McLaren did not start this year in a position to win races, and even with several upgrades, the team has produced a car that is still very tricky to drive. But this weekend in Brazil, Norris demonstrated the form that earned him the number 1.

Rain on Saturday during qualifying helped jumble the usual running order. It was heavy enough that drivers used the wet weather tire with deep treads, which usually generates so much spray that visibility disappears and running has to be suspended. But with fewer cars on track in the later knockout qualifying sessions, this was not a problem for once. More disruption came from grid penalties for Verstappen and Ferrari’s Lewis Hamilton, both of whom needed to use extra power unit components outside of the season’s allowance.

Norris claimed pole, with this year’s new champion, Kimi Antonelli, alongside in his Mercedes. Row two was filled with the unusual sight of Liam Lawson’s Racing Bull and Nico Hulkenberg’s Audi, followed by Red Bull’s Isack Hadjar and Oliver Bearman’s Haas. Next was George Russell in the other Mercedes, then the even more unusual sight of Fernando Alonso’s Aston Martin, normally found much farther back. One could say the same about William’s Alex Albon, who qualified ninth ahead of the second McLaren of Oscar Piastri.

It looked like it was going wrong for Norris right from the start of the race. Antonelli made a better start and passed him into turn 1. Lawson followed through into second place at the next corner. But Norris kept his head down, and by lap six, he was past the Racing Bull, with about five seconds to make up on Antonelli.

Well-timed pit stops saw Norris switch to the right tires at the right time, and he made none of his rivals’ mistakes, even when the rain returned in the final few laps—at least until the podium ceremony, when he tried to fizz his champagne bottle but instead shattered it.

Keeping one’s tires in the right operating window was vitally important in Brazil, but more than a few struggled with it. The Mercedes duo appeared to struggle to generate sufficient tire temperatures, and their cars looked difficult to handle, prompting plenty of complaints from both Russell and Antonelli to the garage. Russell had been progressing well and ran in the top three early in the race but spun after leaving the pit lane for his first stop, ending up facing the wrong way.

A few laps later, Antonelli spun his own car from the lead, this time going into turn 1, letting Norris back into first. Antonelli then did it again four laps later, ending fourth. Eventually, the Mercedes finished with Russell in fourth and Antonelli in fifth. But the team sealed its Constructors’ Championship, so there was still plenty of celebrating despite the relatively mediocre results (by Mercedes’ high standards).

It’s worth watching the in-car footage from the first couple of laps from both Hamilton and Verstappen, who lined up 20th and 21st out of 22 cars. By the end of lap 1, Hamilton was already in ninth and making things look easy—then again, Brazil was his 400th F1 race start, and he has won more than a quarter of them.

But within four laps, his charge had been blunted. Getting his rear tires up to temperature was key to making up so much ground, but with the track drying, the intermediate tires everyone started the race on had a finite life. With his used up, Hamilton started sliding back down the order. On two occasions, he ended up racing his teammate Charles Leclerc, who started mid-pack with his own penalty.

Unlike in Mexico, Italy, or Holland, the two red cars were able to play nice with each other. But neither managed their tires well, and the best they could do on the road was seventh (Leclerc) and ninth (Hamilton), which became eighth for Leclerc after he was given a five-second penalty for rejoining the track unsafely after running off track while battling Lawson.

Verstappen’s first lap wasn’t quite as good as Hamilton’s—the Red Bull was also never entirely within its setup window all weekend. But taking longer to turn on the rear tires probably contributed to their longevity. While Hamilton’s charge through the pack was blunted, Verstappen was in ninth place by lap 4. By lap 24, he was in second, having taken advantage of Antonelli’s second spin. Verstappen would eventually finish the day in second, 14 seconds off Norris.

F1 takes a weekend off next week, then returns for the night race in Las Vegas.

*Perhaps the prime example of this is NASCAR, which switched to a fairly incomprehensible playoff series it has continued to tweak for years in an effort to prevent anyone from winning the big cup until the last race of the year.

Star Wars fans are once again debating one of the saga's biggest plot holes

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Why doesn't Owen Lars recognize C-3PO in A New Hope? I have a theory

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Star Wars fans are once again debating a pesky plot hole in A New Hope, which still doesn't make sense some 49 years later.

In A New Hope, Luke Skywalker and his uncle Owen Lars bring home two droids: R2-D2 and C-3PO. In the scene, the pair appears to be meeting the droids for the very first time. It's revealed in Star Wars: Attack of the Clones, however, that Owen not only knew C-3PO, but that the droid lived in Owen's home for quite some time. So why doesn't Owen recognize him later in Episode 4?

"Owen treating droids like kitchen appliances makes total sense. And Obi-Wan is in witness protection, he's not about to go "hey, it's that Roomba from the Clone Wars," one fan wrote in the r/StarWars subreddit.

"Given that 3PO was probably the equivalent of a vacuum cleaner for the Lars family, that's hardly surprising," wrote another.

"He's a common model. People are saying Anakin built him from scraps, sure. But he didn't invent protocol droids. He just scrapped one together," one fan said.

"How people view droids seem to be very personal. Some view them as pets, others as an assistant, while I think most would just see them as household tools," someone else said. "For example, when you see a Dyson Vacuum, do you ever say, 'That’s my Dyson'?"

However, this is perhaps the most plausible fan theory: "Because contrary to what others may say, Lucas made it up as he went along."

Despite A New Hope being the first movie released in the franchise, it's technically the fourth episode. Star Wars: The Phantom Menace, released in 1999, is Episode 1, with Attack of the Clones serving as Episode 2, and Revenge of the Sith as Episode 3. The most likely reason why Owen doesn't recognize C-3PO is because George Lucas wrote and released A New Hope first... before figuring anything else out by way of backstory. Owen owning C-3PO in Attack of the Clones, which was released in 2002, was probably written with the intention of continuity... but ended up creating a massive continuity error.

The most satisfying in-universe theory, however, is that Owen Lars saw C-3PO as just another household appliance, and therefore didn't recognize him all those years later.

Star Wars: A New Hope will return to theaters in February 2027. Next up for Star Wars is Ahsoka season 2, which arrives on Disney Plus on January 20, 2027. Until then, check out our guide to all the upcoming Star Wars movies and TV shows for everything else that's in the works.

Trump questions midterm election results, revives voting-machine conspiracy

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump on Monday questioned the legitimacy of the midterm election results, resurrecting his pattern of raising doubts about electoral losses for himself and other Republicans.

Trump's social media post raising questions about the election came six days after his fellow Republicans suffered historic defeats at the polls nationwide, including in several GOP strongholds like Texas and South Carolina.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump wrote on Truth Social.

"How could the Polls be so wrong??? Some early voting 'dumps' were so large that it was impossible to win, no matter how many people went to vote," the president said. "It's all about the machines, which are far more important than the voter!"

Trump's post was a sharp pivot from what has been a somewhat conciliatory tone after last Tuesday's thumping of Republican candidates.

"I'll get along probably with the Democrats unless they want to be destructive," Trump said a day after the election.

After his electoral loss to former President Joe Biden in 2020, Trump argued, without evidence, that he only lost as a result of widespread ballot fraud.

Trump's claims, which were joined by dozens of unsuccessful court cases challenging election procedures, culminated in the Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol by a mob of his supporters after he spoke at a rally outside the White House.

The riot disrupted a joint session of Congress for hours. Later that day, Congress confirmed Biden's election by the Electoral College.

Critics of Trump's conspiracy theories about elections have noted that he lost the 2020 race when he was president, but won the 2024 presidential election, when Biden was in the White House.

They also noted that in the past week, Republicans had control of states that saw major Democratic wins that few analysts would have predicted a year ago, including Texas, Kansas, South Carolina, and Iowa.

Mysterious gas on Mars suggests magma below

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Something is leaking from the ground near one of Mars’ largest volcanoes, and scientists think it’s a sign that active magma is buried below.

In a study published Nov. 4 in Science Advances, researchers report detections of hydrogen chloride (HCl) gas within 6 miles (10 kilometers) of the planet’s surface near the volcano Alba Mons. Notably, the detections came during aphelion, when Mars is farthest from the Sun and HCl is typically absent from its atmosphere. The location and out-of-season timing combined with the site’s unique geology likely point to volcanic outgassing — gas escaping from magma as it rises toward the surface — the authors argue.

“It seems that we are showing that there may still be more heat inside Mars than we thought, enough heat to have still fluid magma at depth,” co-author Daniel Mège, a professor at the Space Research Centre of the Polish Academy of Sciences, tells Astronomy.

The detection adds to mounting evidence, including recent detections of marsquakes, that the martian interior is not the dead world scientists once assumed. While scientists know volcanism influenced the planet’s evolution, it has remained unclear whether it continues today.

There’s no question that igneous activity shaped Mars into the planet we see today. Between 3.7 billion and 3 billion years ago, eruptions flooded the planet’s surface with vast lava plains. Later volcanic activity built some of the solar system’s largest volcanoes, including the roughly 16-mile-high (25 km) Olympus Mons — the tallest — and the 4.2-mile-high (6.8 km) Alba Mons, the largest by surface area. Though it rises just 4.2 miles (6.8 km), Alba Mons sprawls roughly 870 by 620 miles (1,400 by 1,000 km).

Those colossal peaks formed not through shifting plate tectonics like on Earth, but through stagnant-lid tectonics. Mars’ outer shell is a single rigid plate, meaning magma repeatedly erupted at the same sites, piling lava to towering heights over millions of years.

Many of these giants, including Olympus and Alba Mons, sit atop Tharsis, a volcanic plateau roughly 3,000 miles (5,000 km) across. Some lava flows at Tharsis are relatively young, dating back only tens of millions of years, yet there has been no definitive evidence for ongoing volcanic activity at the plateau or on the planet as a whole. Nevertheless, the case has been slowly building.

Before its mission ended in 2022, NASA’s InSight lander recorded more than 1,300 marsquakes. Quakes alone don’t prove volcanic activity. “Marsquakes can happen for multiple reasons, only one of which is faulting or other tectonic activity around volcanoes,” says R. Aileen Yingst, a senior scientist at the Planetary Science Institute who studies Mars geology and was not involved in the study. For example, some of the marsquakes InSight detected were caused by meteoroid impacts. And a cooling and contracting crust can cause seismic activity without magma.

Most of the quakes, though, were traced to a system of young volcanic fissures — long, parallel cracks where the martian crust was once pulled apart by rising magma — called Cerberus Fossae. Deposits of volcanic ash at Cerberus Fossae date to as recently as 53,000 years ago. Some scientists argue the large number of marsquakes in this setting could hint that magma is still moving below. In 2022, researchers proposed that a 2,500-mile-wide (4,000 km) active mantle plume lies beneath the Elysium Planitia region that surrounds Cerberus Fossae. On Earth, such columns of hot solid rock rise from deep in the planet’s interior, melting rock and creating magma, which could explain both the marsquakes and the ash deposits.

Orbiters searching for evidence of active volcanism on the martian surface have yet to turn up anything conclusive, the authors say. Sulfur dioxide (SO₂), the gas most often used to track volcanoes on Earth, has never been detected on Mars at concentrations that would point to magma. But that absence doesn’t mean Mars’ magma has gone quiet. Measurements at volcanoes on Earth show that SO₂ dominates during eruptions but drops sharply between them, when HCl becomes the more abundant gas. “[A]s long as we do not observe an active lava flow, or lava fountain, or explosion on Mars, we do not expect SO₂ emissions to be very high,” Mège says. Mars is also about 2.5 times richer in chlorine than Earth, and some martian volcanic meteorites are “up to four times richer” in chlorine than sulfur. “All this suggests that HCl is the best gas to look for on Mars if we want to identify active magma at depth that is not being erupted,” Mège explains.

The detections in the study come from the Atmospheric Chemistry Suite (ACS), a set of three infrared spectrometers aboard the Trace Gas Orbiter, a joint mission of the European Space Agency and Russia’s space agency, Roscosmos. The orbiter began science operations in 2018. ACS’s spectrometers analyze the sunlight filtering through the martian atmosphere, picking out the wavelengths each gas absorbs to reveal what’s in the air and at what altitude. In 2020, ACS made the first detection of HCl on Mars. It appeared planetwide during the warm, dusty season near perihelion, reaching concentrations of 4 to 5 parts per billion. Researchers attributed the presence of that HCl to chemical reactions involving dust, ice, and water vapor in the atmosphere during the warm season, not to volcanic activity.

What’s different about the new finding is that they detected HCl not at perihelion, but near aphelion, when the gas is otherwise absent from Mars’ atmosphere. The HCl was also localized above an interesting geological location: the region around the sprawling, low-profile volcano Alba Mons, located on the northern edge of Tharsis. After two tentative detections in one Mars year, a dedicated search two Mars years later turned up six more, mostly northeast of the volcano. The gas hugged the lowest 6 miles (10 km) of the atmosphere at concentrations up to 1.5 parts per billion.

The team ruled out atmospheric processes for the HCl and considered three possible ground sources: volcanic activity, mud volcanism, and the breakdown of chlorine-bearing salts called perchlorates. Mud volcanoes — likely inactive for billions of years — are widespread across Mars’ northern lowlands, so they alone couldn’t explain why the gas showed up in only one isolated place. And even if mud volcanism were active and releasing the HCl, it would likely still point to magma beneath the surface. Perchlorates are likewise found across the northern lowlands, which rules them out on the same grounds. “[I]f this mechanism were involved, HCl would be detected throughout the northern hemisphere, it would not be local,” Mège says. That leaves localized volcanic activity, which fits with models of the planet’s formation suggesting HCl should be among the most abundant gases in its volcanic emissions.

To gather more data, the team turned to high-resolution images from the High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) camera aboard NASA’s Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter, and the Colour and Stereo Surface Imaging System (CaSSIS) aboard the Trace Gas Orbiter. They were looking for features near Alba Mons that could explain the seepage. They found a grouping of several narrow fissures that had not previously been identified. Many were just tens of centimeters wide, and some extended for up to 6.21 miles (10 km) long. Some were ringed by dark halos, suggesting material may have recently been ejected from within. The fissures were so thin that seasonal dust and ice activity should have already covered them up unless something was actively keeping them open. The authors did not confirm that the HCl came from the fissures, but they suggest the cracks may run from the icy ground near the surface all the way down to magma. Gas rising through them could escape directly or become trapped in the frozen ground, seeping into the atmosphere over time as the ice turns to vapor.

The case isn’t closed. The team didn’t catch gas escaping from any specific fissure. And although the cracks hadn’t been described before, they appear in older orbital images, with no visible changes between then and now. The authors stop short of a firm conclusion, saying only that ongoing volcanic activity is the best explanation for what they found. “What we can say today is: when we consider all the alternatives, it is the best explanation,” Mège notes. The next step is to keep watching. Future searches could reveal more hidden pockets of gas, and with them, more signs that Mars’ interior is still stirring. Ultimately, “to confirm the source,” Mège says, “we would need someone or some robot to check, with a very multidisciplinary payload.”

If there is still magma beneath Alba Mons, the implications reach beyond geology. Mars is smaller than Earth, so it should have shed its internal heat faster. “If it’s the case that Mars is still volcanically active, then the thermal models of Mars would probably need to be revisited,” Yingst explains. And where magma meets groundwater, hydrothermal systems – the kind of environment where life on Earth may have first emerged – usually follow. If conditions stayed stable long enough, Mège says, life could have arisen there. And “if these stable conditions continue until now, then these living organisms may still be there, somewhere below the surface.”

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

Bizarre AI chip demo uses butter to highlight how new chip runs 40 degrees cooler than the competition

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

South Korean chip startup DEEPX says its new AI chip will be at the heart of upcoming Hyundai robots.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Processors generating excess heat play a significant part in contemporary data center design, cost, and resource use. However, things could be vastly different if AI chips were more efficient and ran far cooler. South Korean chip startup DEEPX, recently featured by CNBC, thinks its AI chip designs are the answer. DEEPX demonstrated its DX-M1 processor’s cool-running capabilities in a bizarre new dairy-based thermal benchmark. While rival chips caused a knob of butter to sizzle in seconds, atop the DEEPX solution, the butter block remains solid.

Concerns over AI data center proliferation are growing worldwide. These developments are shaping business, the environment, and politics. Some people have very strong feelings about them, and hundreds of people have risked being thrown into jail protesting data centers. The CNBC report linked in the intro looks into why modern data centers are problematic, and indicates that the core issues stem from the use of hot-running processors and the extensive power and cooling apparatus they need.

DEEPX says its simple answer is to “make the AI chips themselves run cooler.” The CNBC video includes a demo of the ultra-low-power DX-M1 processor. In a side-by-side test against unnamed rival AI chips, you can see the DX-M1 NPU is clearly cooler. DEEPX CEO Kim Nok-won claims a temperature difference of 25 to more than 40 degrees Celsius compared with competitors. Kim said the side-by-side chips processed “the same AI workload… and every chip melted the butter except the last chip [DX-M1].”

The dairy-based thermal challenge is certainly eyebrow-raising, but we note some big gaps in the comparison information. Most notable was the question of how fast each processor tackles the test workload. Anyone can run a chip cool if the power and clocks are scaled back. PC enthusiasts undervolt and/or underclock their GPUs all the time for efficiency, better thermals, and reduced fan noise.

DEEPX’s DX-M1 NPU has been produced on Samsung 5nm since August 2025, according to The Elec reports. It has won approximately $13.3M worth of orders thanks to this sliver of silicon. The company is planning to move forward with the next-gen DX-M2 AI chip, which is based on Samsung 2nm, with its mass production aiming for H2 2027.

In conclusion, these NPUs look set for edge computing and robotics applications, so they aren’t really competing in the same space as the racks of power-gulping Nvidia Blackwell and Ruben GPUs featured in the CNBC news feature. Eventually, we might see the hundreds or thousands of hot-running Nvidia GPUs with beefy liquid cooling apparatus get replaced by cooler-running custom accelerators. Such an innovation could cool the backlash against AI data centers. However, DEEPX’s DX-M1 or even DX-M2 chips aren’t going to compete in that space, so this butter test is a bit of a red herring.

The CNBC report also highlights SK hynix’s work on iHBM, a next-gen low-power consumption, and cool-running. The ‘i’ stands for ‘Integrated Cooling Element,’ according to an exec talking to CNBC. CoolIT is also trying a similar approach by bringing water cooling even closer to the heat-generating silicon.

Djed Spence makes Tottenham return after £25m transfer as reunion photo emerges

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Tottenham, featuring team news and expert analysis from Sam Tabuteau

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Djed Spence has made a return to Tottenham just months after leaving the club.

Sort of. The now-Inter Milan defender has made a comeback to north London as part of England’s Nations League camp for this month’s international break, after he was called up by Thomas Tuchel to be one of the Three Lions’ two left-back options alongside Lewis Hall of Newcastle United.

Spence, who starred at last summer’s World Cup in England’s run to the semi-finals in north America, starting in the 2-1 defeat by Argentina in Atlanta, Georgia.

The 26-year-old made the switch to the Serie A in the transfer window following the tournament, in a £25million deal.

Spurs had already signed Andy Robertson to add to their defensive numbers under Roberto De Zerbi, who oversaw a £380m summer overhaul of his first-team cohort.

Spence’s game time at the Tottenham Hotspur Stadium already looked in jeopardy with Pedro Porro the club’s vice-captain and go-to option at right-back, while versatile midfielder Archie Gray as also deputised ably in that role.

At left-back, Destiny Udogie had established himself as the first-choice pick, despite his persistent injury issues.

Spence’s return to Hotspur Way, his former club’s training ground, was captured on social media, with the full-back taking to his Instagram story to post a photograph posing with three former team-mates.

They were two of Spurs’ injured players - Xavi Simons and Wilson Odobert - and Richarlison, who has been frozen out under De Zerbi after not being registered in the club’s Premier League squad this term.

The Brazilian failed to secure a departure out of the capital - unlike Spence - in the summer, and he has not featured for the club since the transfer window - and the squad registration deadline - slammed shut.

Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Official DLC Teases Majora's Mask Remake

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake has already proven to be a massive success, garnering incredible reviews from critics and fans alike, and almost assuredly amassing the impressive sales numbers Nintendo is expecting. Its faithful recreation of the original, coupled with the handful of innovations it brings to the table, has cemented it as a must-play Nintendo Switch 2 title and a worthwhile exclusive. Suffice it to say, Ocarina of Time's remake is a worthwhile successor to the 1998 classic.

However, as great as Ocarina of Time's remake proved to be, fans are already wondering whether its sequel, Majora's Mask, will be getting the same treatment. It has always seemed extremely likely that Nintendo would remake Majora's Mask, if only because of how connected the two titles are, but it is certainly far from a guarantee. Well, that was until recently, when a seemingly leaked piece of Ocarina of Time DLC appeared to confirm that a Majora's Mask remake is, indeed, on its way.

It seems very likely that Ocarina of Time's remake will be getting some form of DLC in the near future. Various datamines of the game have revealed that fans may finally be getting the Master Quest mode omitted from the base game. Additionally, as spotted by YouTuber Austin John Plays, there appears to be a mask mentioned in the datamined game files that does not appear in the game. It is this mask that seems to point towards a potential Majora's Mask remake.

This isn't the first clue pointing to a Majora's Mask remake, but it is one of the most compelling. In Ocarina of Time's remake's files, there are various lines of dialogue said by certain NPCs whenever Link wears one of the available masks. All of the lines found in the files appear in-game, aside from one regarding mask 103. The dialogue associated with this mask has NPCs refer to it as "weird", "distracting", and making Link look like a "monster". Indeed, the mask's rarity, general creepiness, and association with monsters are brought up frequently.

This has led Austin John Plays and many others to speculate that the mask could be associated with a Skull Kid amiibo, one that would release alongside the Majora's Mask remake in 2028. The amiibo would add Majora's Mask to the game as a wearable mask and would consequently trigger the aforementioned leaked lines of dialogue. It's a logical theory, one that seems fairly rooted in reality considering that remakes appear to be playing a key role in Nintendo's release strategy for the Nintendo Switch 2.

A Majora's Mask remake also feels like an inevitability at this point, both based on the enormous success of the Ocarina of Time remake and the fact that it is that game's direct sequel. While not every remake necessitates a sequel, it would be a shame not to continue this story, especially as the original Majora's Mask utilized a lot of assets from Ocarina of Time, a feat that could be easily replicated now, especially as Nintendo put a lot of effort into the various characters and locations that carry over between games.

Scientists designed a new way to hunt for signs of life in space using electricity and artificial intelligence

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

An electrical sensor told mirror-image amino acids apart, one molecule at a time, taking a step toward small instruments that can look for signs of life.

Your left and right hands are made of the same parts, yet a left hand won’t fit properly into a right glove. Many of the molecules in living things come in the same two mirror-image versions, and life on Earth builds its proteins overwhelmingly from just one of them.

A team led by researchers in Japan has now told those two versions apart, one molecule at a time, using only an electrical current.

The sensor could one day help a small spacecraft instrument look for that lopsided mix on another world. The team also tested it on extracts from a meteorite and from desert soil, where it caught the broad pattern but missed on several details.

Takahito Ohshiro and Masateru Taniguchi of the University of Osaka led the work with colleagues at Georgia Tech, NASA Goddard, MIT and the University of Chicago. Everything so far has happened in a lab, and no flight instrument exists yet.

Amino acids, the small molecules that link up into proteins, mostly come in mirror images, a left-handed form and a right-handed form. Scientists label them L and D. Chemistry without life tends to make the two in equal amounts, while living things lean heavily toward one, the L form.

A strong tilt toward one hand is therefore a possible sign of biology. Some researchers have proposed an excess of more than 20 percent in several amino acids as a threshold that would point to life.

Meteorites once seemed to carry such a tilt, but those results are now generally blamed on contamination from Earth. Samples brought back from the asteroids Ryugu and Bennu showed roughly equal mixes.

In an interview with Earth.com, Taniguchi was careful about what the measurement can show. “Importantly, chirality should not be viewed as proof of life by itself,” Taniguchi said.

“It would become much more powerful when combined with molecular identity, abundance, chemical context, and other biosignatures.”

The sensor starts as a thin gold wire that is pulled until it snaps. The two broken tips sit a little more than half a billionth of a meter apart. That’s more than 100,000 times narrower than a human hair.

A small voltage pushes a trickle of current across that gap. When an amino acid drifts in, the current jumps for a few thousandths of a second, then drops back.

Each jump has a shape, and the L and D forms of the same amino acid leave slightly different shapes. A machine-learning program trained on thousands of these spikes learned to sort them.

“We are not measuring only an average signal from a large population of molecules,” Taniguchi said.

Standard lab methods for telling the two mirror-image molecules apart rely on light or chemical reagents. The electrical approach needs neither. Taniguchi said spacecraft instruments have to be small, sturdy, sensitive and energy-efficient, and dropping the optics could help with that.

The team tested 19 amino acids that come in both forms. Working from a single spike, the program picked the right form about 88 percent of the time on average. The hardest pair, glutamine, came in at 78 percent, and the easiest, isoleucine, at 97 percent.

One spike is a weak basis for a verdict, and a five-minute run collects hundreds. By the team’s calculation, a program that is right 90 percent of the time on each spike would be right 99.9 percent of the time after 16 spikes.

A harder test asked the program to name both the amino acid and its hand from all 39 possible forms at once. It was right about half the time, far better than the 1-in-39 rate that a guess would give.

Mixtures were harder still. In test solutions holding four or five amino acids, some counted shares landed close to the true values. One was off by 56 percent.

Next came real samples. The team ran extracts from a fragment of the Murchison meteorite, supplied by the Smithsonian. It also tested soil from two sites in Chile’s Atacama Desert, a Mars-like environment. A standard lab instrument analyzed the same extracts for comparison.

The program only counted spikes it was confident about, and it rejected about 93 percent of the spikes from a blank sample that contained no extract.

Both methods found glycine to be the most common amino acid in the meteorite, at 48 percent by the sensor and 63 percent by the standard instrument.

Other results drifted. The sensor put serine at nearly 12 percent of the meteorite’s amino acids, compared with well under 1 percent by the standard method. It also found no glutamic acid at all in either desert soil.

For handedness, the sensor matched the standard method on aspartic acid in the meteorite, but not on alanine or valine.

“In that sense, the real samples were not only a demonstration of the technology, but also an important test of its current limits,” Taniguchi told Earth.com.

The authors wrote that the method should be treated as a targeted tool for a shortlist of known molecules, not a full chemical inventory. It can’t yet recognize a molecule it wasn’t trained on, and a messy natural sample adds background signals that blur the counts.

Taniguchi listed what a flight version would need. It would have to measure amounts more accurately, flag unfamiliar molecules, prepare its own samples and keep out contamination from Earth.

It would also need shrunken electronics that survive vibration, radiation and temperature swings.

“Ultimately, we envision a compact instrument in which sample preparation, single-molecule electrical detection, and AI-based molecular identification operate as one system,” Taniguchi said.

Only a working prototype can show whether the sensor works away from a clean lab bench, on a sample from Mars, an icy moon or an asteroid.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Communications.

LiveWire just showed off its biggest electric motorcycle lineup yet

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

LiveWire has spent the last few years expanding beyond the premium electric motorcycles that originally defined the Harley-Davidson spin-off. But seeing the company’s latest machines all together at EICMA in Milan really drives home just how much broader LiveWire’s ambitions have become.

I stopped by the LiveWire booth at EICMA 2026, where the company had everything from its new lightweight S4 Honcho models to the freshly acquired DUST electric dirt bikes – plus STACYC’s electric balance bikes for the youngest riders.

It’s quite a spread, and perhaps more importantly, it paints a picture of a company that increasingly wants to have an electric two-wheeler for just about every stage of a rider’s life.

I also had a chance to spend several hours test-riding the S4 Honcho Street in Milan, so be on the lookout for that article and video to experience it with me.

But for now, let’s see what was at the LiveWire booth.

Perhaps the most important bikes at the booth were the S4 Honcho models, LiveWire’s new lightweight and lower-cost electric motorcycles.

The production S4 Honcho Street made its European debut at EICMA alongside the dirt-oriented S4 Honcho Trail. The Trail version recently began reaching customers in the US, with the Street now en route to dealerships, and European sales are beginning around the EICMA launch.

These bikes represent a significant departure from LiveWire’s earlier strategy. Instead of trying to compete directly with full-size gasoline motorcycles on outright performance, the Honcho platform is aimed at riders looking for something smaller, simpler, and more approachable.

And now the family is getting even bigger.

Also on display was the new S4 Honcho Skate, making its first public appearance.

The Skate is planned for 2027 and further expands the modular Honcho platform with a stripped-down, playful design.

For those who have followed H-D’s electric ambitions for as long as I have, you might even remember the original Harley-Davidson electric moped prototype that seems to have served as inspiration for the Honcho Skate’s design.

Seeing the various Honcho versions together also makes LiveWire’s strategy with the platform much clearer. Rather than creating one small electric motorcycle, the company is building an entire family around the concept.

The other major attraction at LiveWire’s booth was the DUST lineup.

LiveWire acquired the assets of DUST Moto earlier this year, bringing the lightweight electric dirt bike company under its umbrella. EICMA marked the first public showing of the production-intent DUST Hightail under LiveWire ownership.

The Hightail had already generated plenty of interest before the acquisition as a lightweight, high-performance electric dirt bike. Now we’re getting our first look at how that machine fits into LiveWire’s broader plans.

The company is targeting a 2027 introduction for the production Hightail.

LiveWire also showed the new DUST Spur, another model planned for 2027 and intended to expand the newly formed DUST family.

The DUST acquisition is particularly interesting because it gives LiveWire an immediate foothold in the rapidly growing lightweight electric off-road motorcycle space without having to develop an entirely new platform from scratch.

In what could be a hint at faster charging to come, there was an interesting S2 Alpanista Corsa motorcycle that had been outfitted with a NACS plug and DC fast-charging capabilities.

LiveWire claimed a 20-80% charge in under 10 minutes, which would revolutionize the ability to do long-distance motorcycle touring, or just make it more convenient to get a quick charge and head back to your apartment.

There’s on word on whether the concept will make it to production, but the specs are enticing!

The most interesting part of LiveWire’s EICMA display may not have been any individual motorcycle, but rather what happens when you stand back and look at the entire booth.

On one end you’ve got STACYC electric balance bikes. LiveWire says the brand has already introduced more than 250,000 riders to two wheels.

Then you’ve got the lightweight S4 Honcho family. Above that sit the DUST machines for more serious off-road riding, while LiveWire’s existing S2 motorcycles continue to cover the higher-performance street end of the spectrum.

That’s becoming something of an electric two-wheeler ladder.

A kid can theoretically start on a STACYC, graduate into increasingly capable electric two-wheelers, and eventually wind up on a proper electric motorcycle – all within the same corporate family.

And that feels particularly relevant at a show like EICMA, where much of the motorcycle industry is still trying to figure out exactly where electric motorcycles fit.

LiveWire seems increasingly convinced that the answer isn’t simply to build electric replacements for existing gasoline motorcycles.

Instead, the company is spreading out into categories where electric drivetrains can create different kinds of motorcycles altogether.

I’ve been following LiveWire’s evolution for years, and the company standing in front of me at EICMA looks considerably different from the one that started out selling a single expensive flagship electric motorcycle.

The big, high-performance bikes are still important, but machines like the S4 Honcho and DUST Hightail are arguably more interesting to me because they can bring entirely different riders into electric motorcycling.

They’re smaller. They’re simpler. And perhaps most importantly, they’re targeting the kind of fun, accessible riding that doesn’t require superbike levels of performance.

There’s still plenty we need to learn about several of these new models, particularly final specifications and pricing for the 2027 machines.

But seeing the Honcho, Honcho Skate, Hightail, Spur, and STACYC machines together at EICMA makes one thing pretty clear: LiveWire isn’t just trying to sell a few electric motorcycles anymore.

It’s trying to build an entire electric two-wheeler ecosystem.

Manchester United flops hit with ‘non-league’ jibe from Arsenal legend

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Manchester United’s defending against Aston Villa ‘wouldn’t be seen in non-league’ football, says Martin Keown, who asks if the club is ‘sleepwalking into relegation.’

The Red Devils escaped with a point after an incredible 4-4 draw at Old Trafford on Sunday after twice falling behind by two goals.

Alysson and Nicolas Jackson both scored in the opening five minutes, before Manchester United fought back to level before half-time through Bruno Fernandes and Youri Tielemans.

The second half started as badly as the first for the home team, with John McGinn and Leon Goretzka putting the Villains 4-2 up on 53 minutes.

The clock ticked past 90 minutes and that scoreline remained, before substitutes Joshua Zirkzee and Patrick Dorgu scored in injury time to compete a chaotic contest.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

Michael Carrick’s team has won just one of the first 10 Premier League games this season, sitting 18th in the table with eight points accrued.

Keown saw the Manchester United defence as fearful and not at a level anywhere near the Premier League, left with the feeling that relegation is a genuine concern.

‘Are they sleepwalking into relegation? You’re 10 games in, one win. All competitions, 40 goals conceded,’ Keown said on talkSPORT.

‘Defending, you wouldn’t see that in the non-league that I saw yesterday, honestly. The goalkeeper making mistakes, he’s joining in now. I smell fear across that back four.’

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

Keown accepts that Manchester United showed some fight to respond at the death and also did so in their win over Roma earlier in the week, but thinks something is ‘very wrong’ with a team that continues to fall behind in matches.

‘Okay, I saw character and personality and saw it in midweek,’ said the former England defender. ‘But when you win a game like that against Roma, you have to put that in a bottle and channel all that energy from the first whistle against Villa and they were two goals down in each half within minutes.

‘Something is very wrong. The players are not motivated. You can’t keep coming back from the brink in the way that they did. Okay you’ll say that’s a bit of personality, but something is the wrong with the team and the way it defends.’

Carrick was not too down on his team’s performance overall, but insisted that it is just ‘moments’ that are costing Manchester United.

‘There was a lot happened in the game,’ he said. ‘It’s just the moments that are making it [difficult]. We’re kind of beating ourselves a little bit, with the moments that we’re giving away and goals that we’re giving away in tight clusters, that are making it a lot more difficult.

‘It’s hard enough anyway, but making it a lot more difficult than it should be. Football’s about moments. Football’s about what happens in both boxes in the end.

‘I think you take the moments that are totally avoidable, goals that we’re giving away, I actually thought we’ve played pretty well and were the better team for the most part, and I’m disappointed that we still haven’t won the game, to be truthful.

‘I thought we had most of the pressure. I thought we defended well, pressed well. We’re just giving them the moments that shape the game in some ways and then finished strongly and took something out of the game but still disappointing that we haven’t actually won it.’

He added: ‘It’s moments that are really causing us the problem. So if we can deal with that, a little bit of concentration, a little bit of confidence, a little bit of belief, a little bit of mentality, all that comes into making the right decisions

‘I’d say the last three games or so, there’s good things that give us a lot of encouragement that we can definitely win a lot of football matches with, and I feel like we should’ve won today even though we’ve still give too much away.’

Petit Planet’s Gacha System Will Only be For Cosmetics

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Daniel is a lifelong gamer with a love for various genres and gameplay styles who enjoys exploring games and playing from various angles.

HoYoVerse’s bread and butter is, of course, gacha games. From its first big hit in Honkai Impact 3rd to the breakout success of Genshin Impact, Honkai: Star Rail, and Zenless Zone Zero, gacha is what brings home the bacon. The items distributed through these games’ gacha banners usually include characters, weapons, equipment, and other assorted elements that directly impact your experience with the game. If you want to play, you gotta be willing to roll the dice.

With the looming release of Petit Planet, HoYo’s new Animal Crossing-style life sim game, players have been curious as to how, exactly, the gacha element will factor into this particular style of game. It’d be pretty lousy, for example, if you had to keep rolling on banners just to see all of the game’s villagers and experience their stories.

The new life sim gacha game is launching ahead of several other titles this winter.

However, in a departure from its established formula, HoYo’s actually taking a slightly different approach to monetization in Petit Planet. There will still be a gacha system, of course, but the only items this gacha will dispense will be strictly cosmetic, with all of the actual gameplay stuff being readily accessible to everyone whether they’re willing to roll or not.

At the HoYoverse Media Tour 2026 in Seoul, South Korea, two representatives from the Petit Planet dev team, lead game designer Murphy and lead artist Yinli Huang, explained their philosophy to the surrounding press.

In an interview with gaming outlet Geek Culture, Murphy explained that, rather than paywalling access to the game’s content through gacha banners or direct purchases, the goal is to make all of the actual gameplay elements available to all players, with whatever paid elements there being strictly limited to extra cosmetic stuff like character outfits and furniture.

“All of Petit Planet’s core gameplay, including its life simulation and all content related to forming friendships and building stories with the Neighbours, is included for all free players,” Murphy said. “In terms of our monetisation model, we can confirm that it will only be associated with cosmetics, meaning things like furniture and clothes.”

The goal is to make all of the actual gameplay elements available to all players.

This is quite a departure from HoYo’s other major titles. I play Honkai: Star Rail, for example, and because I don’t have every single character and all of their optimal gacha-earned equipment, I’m basically locked out of the game’s hardest endgame game modes.

Wuthering Waves is one of the biggest Gachas on the planet, and they do far better than most at answering player complaints. Let's go over exactly how!

Petit Planet is slated to specifically be a cozy life sim game rather than a management life sim. Many games of this genre purport to be cozy, but because they demand such constant, consistent engagement from you, they kind of stop being cute little daily life simulators and start being second jobs. The dev team is hoping to use the game’s live-service model to offer consistent, interesting content to the players without directly stoking FOMO.

“As a live-service title, we will constantly add new content to the game, like festival events, which are inspired by real-world festivals, alongside the concept of seasons,” Murphy told Geek Culture. “Each season, players will be able to meet new Neighbours, alongside new clothes and furniture to unlock.”

The crux of this pursuit is an as-of-yet undetermined system that would allow players to properly experience and reap the benefits of seasons’ past even if they weren’t playing the game at the time. Again, Star Rail does regular time-limited events, and while they all offer limited-time currency rewards, the actual gameplay elements remain accessible long after their endpoints. Even so, Murphy wants these types of events in Petit Planet to be as low-pressure as possible so players are interested without feeling obligated.

“We always come back to the randomness in daily life, so there’s no growth curve in our game, we just want to provide more of those little surprises and small pleasures in life for players.”

We were able to get some extensive time with HoYoverse's upcoming life simulator, Petit Planet, and were left grinning from ear-to-ear.

A New MFA Design Program Prepares Designers for an Evolving Industry

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Designers today need to do it all. While honing specialized design skills, they are often required to learn marketing, social media strategy, and more. Today’s professionals need to be able to move fluidly between visual communication, product and service design, entrepreneurship, typography, technology, facilitation, research, and cultural strategy—often on the same day. That’s why programs that are designed to help students integrate these modes of practice are so important. At the School of Visual Arts (SVA) in New York City, a new multidisciplinary MFA design program does exactly that.

“Designers today move between media, disciplines, and contexts,” shares Randy Hunt, current SVA MFA Design chair. “Our program prepares them to bring a clear point of view, strong craft, and an understanding of people to the complex opportunities ahead.”

Student work by Julia Hummel. Photography courtesy of the School of Visual Arts (SVA).

Building on SVA’s reputation as a leader in design education, the new MFA design degree is a two-year, multidisciplinary graduate program for designers looking to create meaningful change across industries in products, services, and more. With a powerful tagline, “Design What the World Needs Next,” this degree combines the school’s four graduate-level design programs (Design, Interaction Design, Products of Design, and Design for Social Innovation) into one interconnected degree built for a new era of design.

“Design has always been one of the cornerstones of SVA’s cultural contributions,” Hunt explains. “This next chapter is about preparing designers not only to respond to the world as it changes, but to shape it through rigorous practice, collaboration, critical thinking, experimentation, and meaningful connection to industry and society.”

Photography courtesy of the School of Visual Arts (SVA).

Launching next fall, MFA Design will be based at 136 W 21st Street in the heart of New York City’s bustling Chelsea neighborhood. Led by Randy Hunt, the program features studios, seminars, workshops, as well as critiques, collaborations, and networking opportunities with design professionals—plus access to communal studio spaces, a fabrication and coding lab, and flexible learning spaces. With a curriculum based on the intersection of critical inquiry and practical application, students are asked to research, prototype, develop, and test work that is culturally relevant and resonant.

“SVA’s design programs have always been shaped by working practitioners,” SVA President Christopher Cyphers says. “Our students learn from people who are actively defining the field. The opportunity now is to make those connections even more visible, more integrated, and more powerful across the graduate design experience.”

Photography by ANDREA MIRANDA SALAS & LORENA ESTRELLA (MFA 2020 DSI), courtesy of the School of Visual Arts (SVA).

Grounded in deep industry engagement and SVA’s broader cultural involvement in the city, this multidisciplinary degree underscores the importance of a design education that teaches real-world skills while also setting students up to become creative movers and shakers. Designers don’t just make objects or build buildings; they shape the world we live in.
